The credibility affidavits do not require every reasonable juror to reject the core account, and the medical and childhood submissions do not negate necessary eligibility facts.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No constitutional merits claim is resolved after the gateway fails; the evidence is not found false in every respect, and death is not held mandatory. The accepted procedural classifications and cause determination are not reopened. The prior Caldwell ruling, a new challenge to the wording of an aggravator, and the distinct lower-court competency claim outside this petition remain unadjudicated here.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the Fifth Circuit's refusal of further habeas review. No new factual hearing, sentencing proceeding, trial or release follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Brady v. Maryland, 373 U.S. 83, supplies the underlying suppressed-evidence claim but no merits disposition here. Strickland, 466 U.S. 668, does not allow ordinary sentencing prejudice to replace the gateway. Sawyer v. Smith, 497 U.S. 227, retains its distinct Caldwell retroactivity disposition.

#### [Schlup v. Delo](https://github.com/Alex-Oss222/scotus-career/blob/main/terms/OT1994/records/Schlup_v_Delo_merits_1995-01-23.md)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-7901.
**Decided:** January 23, 1995.

##### Crime innocence invokes a demanding probability gateway

**Holding:** A prisoner seeking to overcome otherwise applicable successive or abusive habeas barriers to independent constitutional claims through innocence of the offense must present reliable new evidence and show, considering the probative record as a whole, that it is more likely than not that no reasonable, properly instructed juror would find guilt beyond a reasonable doubt. This procedural gateway does not itself establish innocence, prove any constitutional violation or shift the ultimate burden on the underlying claims; Sawyer's clear-and-convincing rule remains confined to its distinct death-eligibility setting.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's gateway and all-evidence portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: six Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Murray v. Carrier, 477 U.S. 478: applies and specifies its probability-based crime-innocence exception without changing cause and prejudice.
- Sawyer v. Whitley, 505 U.S. 333: preserves the clear-and-convincing death-eligibility gateway and resolves the crime-innocence question that decision left open; it is not overruled or given a categorical impeachment exclusion.
- McCleskey v. Zant, 499 U.S. 467: preserves successive/abusive-claim barriers and the exceptional innocence route, without treating potential constitutional merit as sufficient to pass it.
- Kuhlmann v. Wilson, 477 U.S. 436: uses the relevant plurality discussion of the whole probative record as persuasive guidance, preserving its actual authority limit.
- Jackson v. Virginia, 443 U.S. 307: distinguishes trial-record legal sufficiency from the likely effect of an augmented record on reasonable jurors; its sufficiency rule remains intact.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The court considers all relevant probative evidence, including evidence excluded at trial or becoming available afterward; trial-admissibility rules do not categorically delimit the gateway record, and even improperly admitted trial evidence may have probative force subject to reliability. The court must assess reliability and likely credibility, not accept every affidavit as true or treat the original verdict as a conclusive answer. Timing, delay and inconsistency may matter; no categorical rule excludes impeachment evidence or discredits a witness solely because the witness is a prisoner. A mere possibility of acquittal, a juror who might disagree, ordinary constitutional-merits prejudice or the reviewing judge's personal reasonable doubt is insufficient. The reasonable juror fairly considers the evidence and follows the beyond-a-reasonable-doubt instruction.

##### This legal error requires a bounded remand despite the statutory refusal permission

**Holding:** The courts' use of the rejected clear-and-convincing crime gateway and reliance on the original jury's credibility function require vacatur and renewed assessment on this record; §2244(b)'s permission to refuse a qualifying subsequent petition does not itself supply an adequate independent ground to sustain that legally mistaken refusal. The Court remands through the Eighth Circuit to the District Court for application of the probability rule and a reasoned determination of necessary factual development, without deciding whether every successful innocence showing compels review, choosing a general appellate standard, or ordering a hearing, writ, retrial, release or stay automatically.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's statutory-refusal and remand portion, joined by O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five Justices. Stone-Zsela supplies a sixth judgment vote without joining the separate §2244(b) rationale.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- McCleskey, 499 U.S. 467: applies the distinction between conditional consideration of new, nonabusive grounds and legally constrained refusal of successive or abusive grounds; neither permission nor exception becomes an unconditional command.
- Keeney v. Tamayo-Reyes, 504 U.S. 1, and Townsend v. Sain, 372 U.S. 293: preserve necessary factual development within their properly presented, available-claim setting and the deliberate-bypass qualification; ordinary counsel negligence alone does not forfeit a required hearing there, but no successive/abusive gateway is eliminated.
- Carrier, 477 U.S. 478: requires use of the selected crime-innocence rule before the present refusal can be sustained on its innocence assessment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Section 2244(b) provides:

> (b) When after an evidentiary hearing on the merits of a material factual issue, or after a hearing on the merits of an issue of law, a person in custody pursuant to the judgment of a State court has been denied by a court of the United States or a justice or judge of the United States release from custody or other remedy on an application for a writ of habeas corpus, a subsequent application for a writ of habeas corpus in behalf of such person need not be entertained by a court of the United States or a justice or judge of the United States unless the application alleges and is predicated on a factual or other ground not adjudicated on the hearing of the earlier application for the writ, and unless the court, justice, or judge is satisfied that the applicant has not on the earlier application deliberately withheld the newly asserted ground or otherwise abused the writ.

The prior-hearing alternatives are a factual evidentiary merits hearing or a legal merits hearing, each followed by a federal denial of release or other habeas remedy to a person held under a state judgment. Both unless conditions must be preserved: the later application must allege and rest on an unadjudicated factual or other ground, and the court must be satisfied that the newly asserted ground was not deliberately withheld or otherwise an abuse. New evidence is not automatically a new ground; absence of deliberate withholding does not eliminate every other kind of abuse. McCleskey requires consideration when both conditions are met and other barriers, including nonexhaustion and procedural default, are absent. The State must plead abuse with clarity and particularity by identifying prior writ history and newly raised claims and alleging abuse before the prisoner's burden arises. Cause and prejudice and the exceptional innocence route remain separate; cause and prejudice is not pressed in this review. Subsection (a)'s separate wording is not inserted into subsection (b), and no new subsection (c) predicate is established.

The videotape was trial evidence. Green's asserted prompt call, Faherty's movement account and other later identification accounts must be assessed with the existing record; the Court finds no witness truthful or false and does not find the gateway satisfied. Proper claim classification, exhaustion, default, abuse, retroactivity and a genuinely sufficient independent merits ground retain their separate operation. Remand is not mandatory constitutional-merits review. Any necessary live testimony is for a reasoned determination under the applicable rules, not an automatic consequence of affidavits or vacatur. The merits of the suppression and ineffective-assistance claims, a freestanding innocence entitlement and any ultimate guilt determination remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Eighth Circuit judgment is vacated and remanded through that court to the District Court for the probability gateway and a reasoned decision on necessary factual development. No gateway satisfaction, automatic hearing, constitutional-merits review, writ, retrial, release or stay is ordered.

### Mootness and completed sentences

#### United States v. R. L. C., 503 U.S. 291 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1577.
**Decided:** March 24, 1992.

For this case’s other questions, see *United States v. R. L. C.*, March 24, 1992, No. 90-1577: whether §5037(c)(1)(b)’s juvenile ceiling includes the applicable adult guidelines maximum (Criminal Procedure — Juvenile sentencing).

##### Whether completion of the reduced juvenile term moots a Government challenge seeking longer detention

**Holding:** Completion of the reduced juvenile detention term does not moot the Government’s challenge when success could restore the longer detention it seeks. The possibility of renewed detention keeps this controversy live.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Souter’s opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; seven Justices adopt the live-controversy determination.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Government seeks restoration of the three-year statutory-maximum approach after the reduced eighteen-month term was served. The threshold ruling neither authorizes renewed detention nor decides the lawful sentencing ceiling.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Court reaches the sentencing-ceiling question. Its affirmance on that question leaves the eighteen-month disposition in place and orders no additional detention.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

*United States v. Villamonte-Marquez*, 462 U.S. 579 — applied to preserve a live controversy where a longer detention may be restored.

### Mootness and decree scope

#### Suter v. Artist M., 503 U.S. 347 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1488.
**Decided:** March 25, 1992.

For this case’s other questions, see *Suter v. Artist M.*, March 25, 1992, No. 90-1488: whether §671(a)(15) creates the asserted individual reasonable-efforts right enforceable through §1983 (Civil Rights — Child-welfare statutory enforcement); whether §671(a)(15) creates a separate implied private action (Civil Rights — Child-welfare statutory enforcement); whether the asserted §671(a)(9) reporting claim is enforceable through §1983 (Civil Rights — Child-welfare statutory enforcement); whether §671(a)(9) creates the asserted separate implied action (Civil Rights — Child-welfare statutory enforcement).

##### Whether the separate B.H. consent decree moots or precludes this statutory-enforcement controversy

**Holding:** The B.H. consent decree neither moots this action nor prevents the officials from contesting the asserted statutory right: its class omits children remaining at home under protective orders, its obligations do not subsume this injunction, and its defined undertaking contains no admission of the independent statutory claim. The Court therefore reaches the private-enforcement questions presented here.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's Opinion of the Court, Part I, joined by the other eight Justices; all nine Justices adopt this threshold ruling.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The B.H. decree remains operative; neither modification nor violation of that decree is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Seventh Circuit’s judgment is reversed as to §671(a)(15) and (9) enforcement through both asserted routes; the three-working-day assignment/reassignment injunction and ancillary weekly monitoring order are vacated, 6–3. Any blanket resolution of the independent §671(a)(16) claim is vacated and that claim remanded for provision-specific consideration, 9–0, without keeping the existing injunction alive on an unadjudicated ground.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Rufo v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail, January 15, 1992, decision — its preservation of consent obligations beyond constitutional minima provides analogous support; its Rule 60(b)(5) standard is not applied to modify B.H., and its holding remains unchanged.

### Mootness and election disputes

#### Norman v. Reed, 502 U.S. 279 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 90-1126 and 90-1435.
**Decided:** January 14, 1992.

##### Whether completion of the election moots the ballot-access dispute

**Holding:** Completion of an election does not moot a ballot-access dispute where election results carry continuing party-qualification consequences and the short election cycle presents a reasonably recurring controversy that otherwise evades review.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's opinion, joined by Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, and Kennedy; these seven Justices supply a majority of the eight participants.

**Material application:** Several candidates exceeded the five-percent threshold that could alter later party qualification. The challenged restrictions operated on a timetable too short for complete review before voting, and the dispute retained concrete consequences.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not revive the unappealed water-district candidates or order a new election.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Moore v. Ogilvie is applied to election restrictions with continuing legal consequences after ballots are counted.

For the party-name and signature holdings in Norman v. Reed, decided January 14, 1992, Nos. 90-1126 and 90-1435, see Election Law, “Ballot access and political parties.”

### Political questions and constitutional review

#### United States Department of Commerce v. Montana, 503 U.S. 442 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-860.
**Decided:** March 31, 1992.

For this case’s other questions, see *United States Department of Commerce v. Montana*, March 31, 1992, No. 91-860: whether equal proportions satisfies the presented interstate population-equality challenge (Election Law — Interstate congressional apportionment); whether count i reversal disposes of the distinct automatic-apportionment claim in count ii (Federal Courts — Scope of appellate disposition); whether count i presents a continuing representation injury redressable by review of the allocation formula (Federal Courts — Standing and representation injury).

##### Whether Congress’s equal-proportions apportionment choice is judicially reviewable

**Holding:** Courts may decide whether the equal-proportions method violates the constitutional requirement to apportion Representatives among States according to their respective numbers. Congress's responsibility to choose a method, and the possibility that several methods satisfy the Constitution, do not make this particular constitutional challenge a nonjusticiable political question.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Opinion of the Court, Part II, joined by the other eight Justices. All nine participating Justices adopt this proposition at its stated scope; it controls by an express majority, with no judgment-only or narrower-ground vote.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The possibility that several constitutional methods exist does not foreclose this constitutional challenge. Baker’s political-question limits in other settings remain; Count II is separate.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse Count I’s judgment, remove its declaration of invalidity and dissolve its permanent injunction, and direct judgment for the Government on Count I only. Remand Count II without deciding it. The allocation remains operative subject to further lawful order; no seat transfer, new enactment or presidential command is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Baker v. Carr, 369 U.S. 186 (1962) — applies its issue-specific political-question inquiry; political subject matter alone does not foreclose constitutional adjudication.
- United States v. Muñoz-Flores, 495 U.S. 385 (1990) — applies ordinary judicial review of constitutional constraints on Congress; its Origination Clause holding is not extended to decide Count II.

### Preservation and question-presented limits

#### Yee v. City of Escondido, 503 U.S. 519 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1947.
**Decided:** April 1, 1992.

For this case’s other questions, see *Yee v. City of Escondido*, April 1, 1992, No. 90-1947: whether restrictions on voluntarily created pad tenancies compel permanent physical occupation (Property and Economic Rights — Physical takings and voluntary tenancies).

##### Whether the unpreserved substantive-due-process claim is reviewable

**Holding:** The Court declines a substantive due-process claim neither properly raised nor decided in the state courts; presentation only in a denied request for discretionary review does not supply the missing state-court decision. The Court need not decide here whether this review limitation is jurisdictional or prudential.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II-A of O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; six Justices adopt this procedural proposition. Stone does not join this rationale.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Due-process merits are not rejected. Whether the review limitation is jurisdictional or prudential is expressly undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the California judgment rejecting the physical-taking claim; award no compensation on that theory. Decline the due-process and regulatory-taking merits without deciding their validity; any properly available regulatory or as-applied claim requires an appropriate proceeding, not a new merits remand here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Illinois v. Gates, 462 U.S. 213 — applied: an unpresented state-court claim is not decided on review; the jurisdictional/prudential classification remains open.
- People v. Triggs, 8 Cal.3d 884 — applied only to the effect of denied California discretionary review, which expresses no merits determination.

##### Whether a new taking argument and this facial theory satisfy preservation and ripeness

**Holding:** A party that properly presents a federal taking claim below may offer a different supporting argument on review, subject to the separate limitation imposed by the question presented. Under the then-governing standards, this facial allegation that the ordinance never substantially advances a legitimate state interest is ripe without a rent-increase request because it does not depend on the particular property’s economic loss or compensation.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II-B–C of O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; six Justices adopt this procedural proposition. Stone does not join this rationale.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding uses the then-governing facial substantial-advancement theory and does not decide regulatory-taking merits or override the separate question-presented limit.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the California judgment rejecting the physical-taking claim; award no compensation on that theory. Decline the due-process and regulatory-taking merits without deciding their validity; any properly available regulatory or as-applied claim requires an appropriate proceeding, not a new merits remand here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bankers Life & Casualty Co. v. Crenshaw, 486 U.S. 71 — applied: new arguments may support a preserved federal claim.
- Illinois v. Gates, 462 U.S. 213 — applied: argument and claim preservation are distinct.
- Williamson County Regional Planning Commission v. Hamilton Bank, 473 U.S. 172 — distinguished: the asserted facial theory does not require a property-specific rent determination.
- Agins v. Tiburon, 447 U.S. 255 — applied only to the then-current facial-ripeness proposition; no merits determination follows.
- Keystone Bituminous Coal Assn. v. DeBenedictis, 480 U.S. 470 — applied to distinguish facial allegations from property-specific application.

##### Whether the physical-taking question fairly includes regulatory-taking merits

**Holding:** Under Rule 14.1(a), a question asking whether the ordinance causes a physical taking does not fairly include the related but independent question whether it causes a regulatory taking. No exceptional urgency or economy justifies departing from that prudential limit here, so the regulatory merits remain undecided.

**Proposition-level authority:** O’Connor, II-D, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas: seven votes; this section adopts no regulatory merits or ripeness conclusion.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Preserving a general taking claim does not enlarge the grant. Physical and regulatory taking questions are related but not subsidiary to one another, and no exceptional urgency or economy warrants enlargement here.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the California judgment rejecting the physical-taking claim; award no compensation on that theory. Decline the due-process and regulatory-taking merits without deciding their validity; any properly available regulatory or as-applied claim requires an appropriate proceeding, not a new merits remand here.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Stone v. Powell, 428 U.S. 465 — applied: departure from the question-presented rule remains exceptional.
- Lytle v. Household Manufacturing, Inc., 494 U.S. 545 — applied: developed arguments and lower-court consideration support disciplined review.
- American National Bank & Trust Co. v. Haroco, Inc., 473 U.S. 606 — applied by analogy: related questions are not necessarily fairly included within one another.

### Preservation and questions passed upon below

#### United States v. Williams, 504 U.S. 36 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1972.
**Decided:** May 4, 1992.

For the other questions in *United States v. Williams*, May 4, 1992, No. 90-1972, see Federal Courts — Preservation and questions passed upon below (reviewability) and Criminal Procedure — Grand-jury presentation and supervisory dismissal (disclosure duty and dismissal authority).

##### Whether the Court may review a federal question passed upon below despite failure to renew an objection to circuit precedent

**Holding:** The Court may review an important federal question expressly decided by the court of appeals even though a party did not renew its challenge to binding circuit precedent in this case, where adherence to that precedent did not concede its correctness. This is a permissible exercise of review, not an obligation to decide every unpressed question or a rule excusing an affirmative waiver.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone’s reviewability opinion, joined by White, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter: five Justices adopt the rule and explanation. The four Justices who prefer dismissal of the writ do not join this rule.

**Material application:** The Tenth Circuit expressly applied Page. Adhering to binding precedent did not concede its correctness; the Government had opposed the rule when it was established.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This discretionary review does not displace trial-objection requirements, excuse affirmative waiver or decide review standards for state judgments.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Court reaches the question, 5–4: Stone, White, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor and Thomas prefer dismissal of the writ.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Stevens v. Department of Treasury, 500 U.S. 1 — applied: actual decision below can satisfy the pressed-or-passed-upon practice.
- United States v. Page, 808 F.2d 723 — its binding force within the Tenth Circuit explains the litigation posture; adherence does not establish agreement with its correctness.

### Prospective equitable relief

#### Rufo v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail; Rapone v. Inmates of Suffolk County Jail, 502 U.S. 367 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 90-954 and 90-1004.
**Decided:** January 15, 1992.

**Companion or consolidated matters:** Rufo and Rapone are consolidated petitions presenting the same modification request; both judgments are vacated.

##### What change justifies modification of an institutional consent decree under Rule 60(b)(5)

**Holding:** Under Rule 60(b)(5), the party seeking to modify an institutional consent decree must prove a significant change in fact or law that makes continued enforcement inequitable; an unforeseeable catastrophe is not required. Actual anticipation and acceptance of the relevant risk impose a heavy burden to justify relief, and dissatisfaction, a new administration, or the availability of a cheaper constitutional alternative alone does not suffice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five of eight participating Justices support the exact proposition. O'Connor concurs in the judgment without joining this rationale; Thomas did not participate.

**Material application:** Officials reaffirmed the single-occupancy obligation in 1985 after the original 1979 decree. The centrality of that term and acceptance of known risks matter, but the lower court must assess inequity without treating unforeseeability as an absolute condition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Population growth, expense, and the need for the proposed design are asserted grounds, not Supreme Court findings satisfying the burden.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Both First Circuit judgments are vacated and the cases remanded for the district court to determine whether proven changed circumstances make continued enforcement inequitable and whether the proposed modification responds suitably to those changes. The existing decree remains operative until lawfully modified.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *United States v. Swift & Co.*, 286 U.S. 106 — its disposition is preserved; its demanding factual formulation is confined to its setting rather than imposed universally.
- *System Federation No. 91 v. Wright*, 364 U.S. 642 — applied to preserve equitable power to adjust continuing obligations to changed conditions.
- *United States v. United Shoe Machinery Corp.*, 391 U.S. 244 — applied to connect modification to the continuing decree's purpose and operation.
- *Board of Education v. Dowell*, 498 U.S. 237 — reinforces contextual treatment of institutional equitable relief; no school-desegregation termination rule is imported.

##### What fit and protections a justified consent-decree modification must preserve

**Holding:** A justified modification must be suitably tailored to the proven change and preserve lawful protection of the decree's remaining purposes; the court may not simply rewrite a consent decree to the constitutional minimum. Bell's clarification of constitutional limits does not by itself eliminate a voluntarily accepted single-cell obligation, and financial pressure cannot authorize unconstitutional confinement.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Scalia, Kennedy and Souter; five of eight participating Justices support the exact proposition. O'Connor concurs in the judgment without joining this rationale; Thomas did not participate.

**Material application:** The district court may consider practical administration and cost in selecting lawful relief, while exercising its own equitable judgment; these considerations cannot replace proof of a qualifying change. Bell does not dissolve the officials' independently accepted single-cell promise.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court approves no double-cell design or number of cells and makes no determination that the proposed arrangement is constitutional or that remaining safeguards are adequate.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Local No. 93, International Association of Firefighters v. Cleveland*, 478 U.S. 501 — consent obligations may exceed what constitutional liability alone would require.
- *Bell v. Wolfish*, 441 U.S. 520 — its constitutional treatment of double-celling does not erase a consensual single-cell requirement.
- *System Federation No. 91 v. Wright*, 364 U.S. 642 — preserves adjustment for changed circumstances while maintaining judicial control of lawful relief.

#### Morales v. Trans World Airlines, Inc., 504 U.S. 374 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1604.
**Decided:** June 1, 1992.

For the direct-reference and independently sufficient fare-marketing-effects grounds of preemption in *Morales v. Trans World Airlines, Inc.*, June 1, 1992, No. 90-1604, see Constitutional Structure — Federal preemption.

##### Whether threatened fare-guideline enforcement permits federal prospective relief before the airlines expose themselves to repeated sanctions

**Holding:** Federal prospective relief may protect a party against imminent state enforcement forbidden by federal law when repeated serious sanctions make awaiting enforcement an inadequate practical remedy. Texas's announced enforcement of these fare guidelines presents that condition; preemption alone does not dispense with the required threatened injury.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas; five of eight participants. Stone states a compatible equitable ground separately without joining the opinion; Souter does not participate.

**Material application:** Texas formally announced intended suits. Continuing the advertisements would accumulate substantial liability, while suspending them during a test case and review would surrender the challenged conduct. Neither option provides an adequate practical substitute on this record.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Texas did not argue *Younger* abstention, which is not decided. A materially different threat still requires its own adequate-remedy and threatened-injury showing; no general exception for every preemption claim is created.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Retain declaratory and injunctive protection against the specified fare-guideline enforcement, subject to the separate scope limitation below.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Ex parte Young*, 209 U.S. 123, applies to imminent unlawful enforcement with repeated sanctions and no adequate practical test-case alternative. *O'Shea v. Littleton*, 414 U.S. 488, preserves adequate-remedy and threatened-injury requirements. *Younger v. Harris*, 401 U.S. 37, is not modified because abstention is not presented.

##### Whether the injunction may extend beyond the unlawful enforcement actually threatened

**Holding:** A preventive injunction against state enforcement must be confined to the unlawful enforcement actually threatened; repeating a federal preemption clause does not justify protection against hypothetical future applications. The decree's coverage of unthreatened airline advertising and operations exceeds the established controversy.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, O'Connor, Kennedy and Thomas; five Justices. All eight participants vote to remove the excess, but Stone, Blackmun and Stevens supply no additional joins to this rationale.

**Material application:** The enforcement notices concern fare advertising, not the entire range of airline operations covered by the decree. The decree would otherwise adjudicate state applications before the State asserted them.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Frequent-flyer programs, boarding compensation and other unthreatened practices are not adjudicated merely because the NAAG materials discuss them. Extension of relief to 33 other Attorneys General and the separately petitioned personal-jurisdiction dispute in No. 90-1606 are outside this review. No damages, immunity for other airline conduct or new territorial extension is ordered.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the excess injunction and remand for narrowing to the specified fare-guideline controversy, retaining the declaratory and injunctive protection supported by the actual threat.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** *Public Service Commission of Utah v. Wycoff Co.*, 344 U.S. 237, excludes hypothetical regulatory disputes from preventive relief. *Ex parte Young*, 209 U.S. 123, supplies the imminent-enforcement boundary, and *O'Shea v. Littleton*, 414 U.S. 488, excludes conjectural injury.

#### Lee v. Weisman, 505 U.S. 577 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1014.
**Decided:** June 24, 1992.

For the Establishment Clause grounds in *Lee v. Weisman*, June 24, 1992, No. 90-1014, see First Amendment — Official school prayer.

##### Whether continued exposure at a forthcoming graduation preserves a live claim for prospective relief

**Holding:** A student who remains in the same public-school system and faces its challenged official prayer policy at her forthcoming high-school graduation has a live claim for prospective protection although her earlier graduation is over. Deborah Weisman's continued exposure permits review and preservation of the injunction without deciding her father's taxpayer standing.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Blackmun's opinion, joined by Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; six Justices adopt the threshold proposition.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated for the student's continuing prospective exposure.

**Material application:** Deborah remains enrolled at Classical High School, and the stipulated record makes clergy-led prayer at her later graduation likely. Her relationship to that forthcoming policy application is concrete; formal optional attendance does not remove it.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court decides no taxpayer-standing rule and creates no general advisory-jurisdiction exception for completed school controversies that might recur.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Preserve review and the injunction against the challenged official practice. The injunction's substantive scope follows the First Amendment holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Engel v. Vitale, 370 U.S. 421, and School District of Abington Township v. Schempp, 374 U.S. 203, supply the substantive school-prayer interest without supplying a new taxpayer-standing rule.

### Ripeness of future statutory obligations

#### New York v. United States, 505 U.S. 144 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 91-543, 91-558 and 91-563.
**Decided:** June 19, 1992.

The consolidated matters are New York, County of Allegany and County of Cortland. For the responsibility-clause, incentive, compulsory-title, Guarantee Clause and severability holdings in *New York v. United States*, June 19, 1992, these three dockets, see Constitutional Structure — Federal incentives and compulsory state administration.

##### Whether the 1996 take-title obligation is ripe for review while it requires present siting decisions

**Holding:** A challenge to a future statutory obligation is ripe where the legal issue is fit for decision and avoiding that obligation requires substantial present action over many years. New York may challenge the 1996 take-title command now because its siting choices must be made now and waste production will continue.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas: five of nine, controlling. Stone-Zsela agrees separately without a formal join.

**Material application:** Developing a disposal site takes years; avoiding the future consequence requires present preparations, and no party suggests the State's generators will stop producing waste. Waiting would not sharpen the legal question and would require the very preparations challenged.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The ruling does not relax concrete-injury requirements, permit review of every distant possibility or revive the abandoned due-process and Eleventh Amendment claims.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The constitutional challenge to the future obligation is heard now; component-specific relief appears at its primary Constitutional Structure location.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Abbott Laboratories v. Gardner, 387 U.S. 136 (1967): applied as the received fitness-and-hardship framework; no standing relaxation follows.
- Pacific Gas & Electric Co. v. State Energy Resources Conservation and Development Commission, 461 U.S. 190 (1983): applied in its presently consequential future-obligation setting, as reproduced in the reviewed report.
- Regional Rail Reorganization Act Cases, 419 U.S. 102 (1974): applied; a future statutory obligation can warrant present review when it already requires consequential action, within the passage reproduced in the reviewed report.

### Scope of appellate disposition

#### United States Department of Commerce v. Montana, 503 U.S. 442 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-860.
**Decided:** March 31, 1992.

For this case’s other questions, see *United States Department of Commerce v. Montana*, March 31, 1992, No. 91-860: whether equal proportions satisfies the presented interstate population-equality challenge (Election Law — Interstate congressional apportionment); whether congress’s equal-proportions apportionment choice is judicially reviewable (Federal Courts — Political questions and constitutional review); whether count i presents a continuing representation injury redressable by review of the allocation formula (Federal Courts — Standing and representation injury).

##### Whether Count I reversal disposes of the distinct automatic-apportionment claim in Count II

**Holding:** Reversal of the judgment and relief resting on Count I does not dispose of Count II's separate challenge to automatic decennial apportionment. Because the lower court left that claim's merits undecided and it is not necessary to resolve the presented Count I questions, Count II is remanded without a Supreme Court decision on its standing, justiciability, merits, or any further relief.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Opinion of the Court, Part IV, joined by the other eight Justices. All nine participating Justices adopt this proposition at its stated scope; it controls by an express majority, with no judgment-only or narrower-ground vote.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Count II’s standing, justiciability, merits and relief remain open. Singleton supplies ordinary remand practice with discretionary exceptions, not a categorical jurisdictional bar to first-instance appellate decision.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse Count I’s judgment, remove its declaration of invalidity and dissolve its permanent injunction, and direct judgment for the Government on Count I only. Remand Count II without deciding it. The allocation remains operative subject to further lawful order; no seat transfer, new enactment or presidential command is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Singleton v. Wulff, 428 U.S. 106 (1976), unanimous Part III — applies its ordinary remand practice and fair-opportunity rationale; its recognized discretionary exceptions remain available, and no new jurisdictional prohibition is announced.

#### [Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton](https://github.com/Alex-Oss222/scotus-career/blob/main/terms/OT1994/records/Vernonia_School_District_47J_v_Acton_merits_1995-06-26.md)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 94-590.
**Decided:** June 26, 1995.

For the other questions in Vernonia School District 47J v. Acton, decided June 26, 1995, No. 94-590. see Criminal Procedure — Student searches and athletic drug testing (Urine testing is a search; athletic participation is no waiver; This interscholastic-athlete testing program is reasonable; The facial prescription-verification objection does not invalidate the policy).

##### Federal correction requires reconsideration of the Oregon ground

**Holding:** The Ninth Circuit judgment is vacated and remanded because its Oregon constitutional conclusion expressly followed from its erroneous Fourth Amendment conclusion. The lower court must reconsider the Oregon question independently; this judgment neither decides Oregon constitutional protection nor unconditionally reinstates final dismissal of the action.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV of Scalia's Opinion of the Court, joined by Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices adopt this disposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Review is of a federal appellate judgment under section 1254(1), not a state high court under section 1257. The Oregon conclusion expressly depended on the federal error, but Oregon need not adopt only the federal minimum.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- New Jersey v. T.L.O., 469 U.S. 325: supplies only the federal search framework; it does not decide the independent Oregon constitutional question.
- No earlier Supreme Court decision is overruled or extended by this bounded federal-appellate remedy.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the Ninth Circuit judgment and remand for independent reconsideration of the Oregon constitutional question after correction of its federal premise. No unconditional reinstatement of final dismissal, athletic-eligibility remedy, damages, refund, drug finding, program redesign or reopening of the rejected school-authority issue follows.

### Scope of prospective relief for abortion requirements

#### Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey, 505 U.S. 833 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 91-744 and 91-902.
**Decided:** June 29, 1992.

For the other questions in Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey, June 29, 1992, Nos. 91-744 and 91-902, see Civil Rights — Reproductive liberty and constitutional precedent (retained protection, governing framework and timetable), Abortion emergency care, Abortion informed consent, Minor consent and independent authorization, Spousal control of reproductive choice, and Abortion reporting and public disclosure; Federal Courts — Scope of prospective relief for abortion requirements; and First Amendment — Abortion information and compelled speech. Each question has one primary entry.

##### No additional materials remand or interim injunction

**Holding:** The record does not sufficiently identify an unlawful state communication or compulsory distribution practice to justify the requested additional materials vacatur, focused remand or interim injunction. The facial dispositions stand at their stated scopes, while a concrete misleading or coercive implementation remains challengeable.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 9 direct rationale joins including the author.

**Operative remedy or transition:** No additional materials vacatur, focused remand or interim injunction is ordered. Ordinary proceedings implement the actual component judgments; concrete misleading or coercive implementation remains challengeable.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Brockett v. Spokane Arcades, 472 U.S. 491 (1985): applied to require relief corresponding to the demonstrated defect while preserving lawful operation.

##### The parental-attendance objection and the denial of special relief

**Holding:** The Court denies an attendance-specific vacatur, remand or interim injunction, 9–0. Five Justices rest on the absence of an established enacted or authoritative compulsory-visit command. Four additionally would sustain the assumed operation with the available bypass. Only the first ground commands a majority.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion, joined by Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter: 5 direct rationale joins including the author. White, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas concur in the judgment on their additional assumed-operation ground.

**Material application:** District findings 232 and 238–259 describe anticipated attendance practices and burdens; Third Circuit footnote 23 reserves the statutory correctness of that interpretation. They do not establish an enacted or authoritative additional-visit command. A later actual duty requires its own burden, alternative and justification assessment; the existence of bypass is not an automatic cure.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Deny special attendance vacatur, remand and interim injunction. The four-Justice assumed-operation rationale does not become an independently sufficient Court holding.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bellotti, Hodgson and Ohio v. Akron: preserve effective independent authorization at their actual scope.
- The general six-step standard: an additional legal obligation must first be established; no invented attendance condition is approved.

#### [Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer](https://github.com/Alex-Oss222/scotus-career/blob/main/terms/OT1994/records/Fargo_Womens_Health_Organization_v_Schafer_merits_1995-02-13.md)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-1712.
**Decided:** February 13, 1995.

For the other questions in this decision, see Civil Rights — Abortion access and provision-specific review (The complete Casey standard governs the access and facial challenge; The abortion definition excludes an unintended diagnostic termination; The specified emergency terms supply a prospective clinical criterion; A doubtful default-fault rationale does not invalidate the general penalty by itself).

##### The access claims return for a supported assessment and prompt interim consideration

**Holding:** The access portion of the Eighth Circuit judgment is vacated, and that court must remand for the District Court to apply Casey to the challenged access duties on a properly supported record. The District Court must promptly consider the providers' request for interim protection under the applicable ordinary standard and current record; this Court neither reinstates the dissolved injunction automatically nor orders a final injunction, hearing or trial.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter's Part V on access remand and prompt interim consideration, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg and Breyer: six Justices.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey, 505 U.S. 833 (1992): applies Step 6's matched relief and preserves its distinction between an actual duty and assumed administration; its case-specific denial of additional relief is no universal bar.
- Interstate Commerce Commission v. Transcon Lines, decided January 10, 1995: applies the distinction among remedial authority, violation and scope by analogy, without transferring its admitted public-enforcement violation or statutory injunction predicates.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Final statewide invalidation, the ultimate State justification, disputed factual burdens, a definitive state severance, actual criminal or civil culpability, damages, a compelled evidentiary hearing, and a new parental, speech or emergency-access claim are not decided. Affirmance of the specified vagueness grounds does not approve a materially different state construction or concrete enforcement.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Access review is vacated and remanded, 6–3; the abortion-definition, emergency-definition and associated limited general-penalty rejections are each affirmed, 8–1. The District Court must promptly consider interim protection under the ordinary standard and current record. No dissolved injunction is automatically restored, no final injunction or hearing is ordered, and no emergency-access claim unpressed below or new parental or speech claim is decided.

### Standing and original jurisdiction

#### Wyoming v. Oklahoma, 502 U.S. 437 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 112, Original.
**Decided:** January 22, 1992.

For the separate questions whether the coal quota violates the Commerce Clause and whether it can survive solely as a public-utility purchasing directive, see *Wyoming v. Oklahoma*, January 22, 1992, No. 112, Original, under Constitutional Structure — Interstate commerce.

##### Whether Wyoming's severance-tax loss establishes its own Article III injury

**Holding:** A documented loss of severance-tax revenue caused by a mandatory substitution away from the State's taxed coal establishes Wyoming's own Article III injury where the record supports causation and an injunction would remove the challenged purchasing mandate. Redressability does not require a guarantee that every displaced purchase will return, but generalized hopes of future tax growth do not suffice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; seven Justices support this proposition.

**Material application:** The purchasing record, the extraction tax, and evidence of available production capacity support an inference of lost severance receipts from compelled substitution away from Wyoming coal. The fiscal inference is supported on the summary-judgment record; it is not itself a stipulated fact.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Ordinary injury, causation and redressability requirements apply. No sovereign exemption or standing based on unrelated future fiscal effects is established.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Maryland v. Louisiana*, 451 U.S. 725 — applied to a State's demonstrated injury from another State's commercial restriction.
- *Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission*, 432 U.S. 333 — geographic market exclusion is a concrete commercial injury; Wyoming still proves its own fiscal stake.
- *Warth v. Seldin*, 422 U.S. 490, and *Simon v. Eastern Kentucky Welfare Rights Organization*, 426 U.S. 26 — ordinary personal-injury and causation requirements are preserved, not relaxed for States.

##### Whether this interstate fiscal controversy warrants exercise of original jurisdiction

**Holding:** The Court exercises original jurisdiction over this substantial interstate controversy because Wyoming's own fiscal injury and the challenged statewide mandate warrant direct resolution and no equivalent pending private action would fully resolve the State's claim. Exclusive jurisdiction under §1251(a) does not eliminate the Court's established discretion in administering its original docket.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; seven Justices support this proposition.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The possibility of a producer's or utility's suit does not require surrender of this distinct state injury, but the holding does not promise an original forum for every indirect effect on tax receipts. Acceptance of the bill does not bar jurisdictional reconsideration on the developed record.

**Material judgment:** Original jurisdiction is exercised by Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter. Scalia and Thomas would dismiss and do not reach the Commerce Clause merits.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *Massachusetts v. Missouri*, 308 U.S. 1 — applied for seriousness of the sovereign interest and adequacy of other available relief.
- *Texas v. New Mexico*, 462 U.S. 554 — preserved for administration of substantial interstate original controversies.
- *Illinois v. City of Milwaukee*, 406 U.S. 91 — alternative forums remain relevant; no equivalent proceeding resolves this State's injury here.
- *Arizona v. California*, 460 U.S. 605 — an earlier jurisdictional ruling is not an irrevocable bar to reconsidering jurisdiction on the developed record.

#### United States v. Alaska, 503 U.S. 569 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 118, Original.
**Decided:** April 21, 1992.

For the other questions in *United States v. Alaska*, April 21, 1992, No. 118, Original, see Federal Courts — Standing and original jurisdiction (live title controversy), Constitutional Structure — Federal and state submerged-land boundaries (§10 condition), and Administrative Law — Coastal permits and Administrative Procedure Act review (regulatory and APA objections).

##### Whether the Nome title controversy remains live after the proposed mineral sale receives no bids

**Holding:** Continuing, adverse federal and state claims to the submerged lands affected by the Nome disclaimer present a live original controversy even though the identified mineral sale received no bids. The Court reaches the parties' cross-motions through its original jurisdiction.

**Proposition-level authority:** White's opinion, Part I, joined by the other eight Justices; all nine Justices support this threshold holding.

**Material application:** The disclaimer reserves legal review, and continuing adverse sovereign claims concern present entitlement rather than proceeds from a particular lease.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Adverse present title claims persist without sale revenue. No abstract dispute over other permits or precise previously disputed coastline is adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Federal summary judgment is granted and Alaska’s competing motion and general injunction request are denied. The declaration enforces the disclaimer only as to boundary change and corresponding entitlement attributable to the permitted Nome extension. Existing state property, claims and disputes over the precise preproject line remain intact; the permit and construction are not vacated. No remand, monetary award, nationwide permitting injunction or surrender of unrelated lands is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

This threshold holding applies Article III, §1251(b)(2), and the stipulated continuing title dispute; it changes no earlier jurisdictional rule.

### Standing and personal injury

#### Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, 504 U.S. 555 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1424.
**Decided:** June 12, 1992.

##### What evidence of Article III injury, causation and redressability is required at summary judgment

**Holding:** A plaintiff invoking federal jurisdiction must establish a concrete and particularized, actual or imminent injury, a causal connection fairly traceable to the defendant rather than independent third parties, and a likelihood that the requested relief will redress the injury. At summary judgment, the plaintiff must supply specific facts by affidavit or other evidence on the challenged elements; favorable reasonable inferences remain available, but allegations cannot substitute for the missing evidence.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; five Justices adopt the elements and litigation-stage evidentiary requirements. Stone concurs in the judgment without joining this section.

**Material application:** Earlier survival of the pleading stage does not establish the specific facts needed at summary judgment. The Court resolves this action on insufficient threatened personal injury.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The general redressability element controls. Its application to absent funding agencies and uncertain foreign-project responses appears only in the three-Justice Part III-B and is not controlling. Favorable reasonable inferences remain available; the decision does not replace Rule 56 or the separate APA programmatic-review holding of National Wildlife Federation.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The merits declaration, mandatory rulemaking relief and dependent §1540(g)(4) fee award are vacated, and the action is remanded for jurisdictional dismissal. Six Justices support dismissal for lack of standing; seven support reversal, with Stevens relying on a noncontrolling statutory ground. No independent fee-entitlement rule or substitute award is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Warth v. Seldin, 422 U.S. 490 (1975): applied for the plaintiff's personal stake and burden to establish standing; its statutory-rights discussion remains subject to concrete injury.
- Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737 (1984): applied for the constitutional elements and the limits on generalized judicial supervision.
- Simon v. Eastern Kentucky Welfare Rights Organization, 426 U.S. 26 (1976): applied for fair traceability and likely redress; no new majority application concerning these absent agencies follows.
- Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation, 497 U.S. 871 (1990): applied for the evidentiary distinction between pleading and summary judgment; its separate APA programmatic-review holding is not enlarged.
- Celotex Corp. v. Catrett, 477 U.S. 317 (1986), and Anderson v. Liberty Lobby, Inc., 477 U.S. 242 (1986): applied to require evidence of challenged essential elements while preserving genuine material disputes and reasonable inferences.

##### Whether past visits, indefinite return intentions and general wildlife interests establish imminent personal injury

**Holding:** Aesthetic, recreational and professional interests in observing wildlife can support standing, but these plaintiffs must show threatened interference with their own concrete use. Prior visits and an indefinite intention to return, without concrete plans or another supported basis for imminent renewed exposure, do not establish standing for these prospective claims; an ecosystem, animal or professional connection that supplies only general interest in distant species does not cure that defect.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III-A of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with five votes. Stone's separate concurrence supplies a sixth vote for the record-specific personal-injury conclusion without a formal opinion join.

**Material application:** The affidavits describe visits to Egyptian and Sri Lankan project areas but no sufficiently definite return undertaking or continuing activity showing imminent exposure. General ecosystem, animal and professional connections supply no missing evidence of these members' own threatened use.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No purchased-ticket or particular-document prerequisite is imposed. Actual use of an affected ecosystem and demonstrated injury transmitted across geographic distance remain undecided. Aesthetic, recreational and professional harm remains cognizable when it affects the claimant's own concrete activity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Jurisdictional dismissal leaves the statute's geographic reach unresolved and does not preclude a later action supported by sufficient standing facts.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Sierra Club v. Morton, 405 U.S. 727 (1972): applied; aesthetic injury is cognizable, but an organization's special interest cannot replace injury to a member.
- Hunt v. Washington State Apple Advertising Commission, 432 U.S. 333 (1977): applied to require an injured member for this associational claim; no other associational requirement is changed.
- Los Angeles v. Lyons, 461 U.S. 95 (1983): applied for the distinction between past exposure and a present or imminent injury supporting prospective relief.
- Whitmore v. Arkansas, 495 U.S. 149 (1990): applied for actual or imminent rather than conjectural injury.
- Lujan v. National Wildlife Federation, 497 U.S. 871 (1990): applied to specific affected use; no categorical bar to geographically transmitted injury is established.
- Japan Whaling Association v. American Cetacean Society, 478 U.S. 221 (1986): distinguished because its whale-watching and studying members alleged an adverse effect on their own activities; concrete professional or ecological injury remains possible.

##### Whether ESA citizen-suit permission and an asserted procedural right replace threatened concrete injury

**Holding:** ESA §11(g)'s authorization for any person to sue does not give Article III standing to a plaintiff asserting only the public's common interest in lawful administration. A procedural right may protect a plaintiff's threatened concrete interest without requiring proof that the procedure will certainly change the ultimate agency decision, but the procedural label cannot replace that personal interest; these respondents have not shown the required connection.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV of Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; five Justices adopt the citizen-suit and concrete-interest rule. Stone does not join this section.

**Material application:** Section 11(g), 16 U.S.C. §1540(g), permits enforcement but identifies no concrete injury to these members independent of the unsupported future use. The public's common interest in lawful administration cannot supply the missing personal connection.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Congress may recognize concrete factual injuries previously inadequate in law; the outer reach of that power is not settled. Procedural protections tied to a threatened concrete interest remain cognizable without certainty of a changed ultimate decision. Neither §7(a)(2)'s geographic reach nor the binding effect of a revised rule on absent agencies is decided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The action is dismissed for want of Article III standing; no new consultation rule or cancellation of any foreign project is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Fairchild v. Hughes, 258 U.S. 126 (1922): applied to reject a general right to compel lawful administration as a sufficient personal injury.
- Massachusetts v. Mellon, 262 U.S. 447 (1923), and Ex parte Levitt, 302 U.S. 633 (1937): applied for the distinction between direct injury and an undifferentiated public grievance.
- United States v. Richardson, 418 U.S. 166 (1974), and Schlesinger v. Reservists Committee to Stop the War, 418 U.S. 208 (1974): applied to generally shared governmental grievances without distinctive concrete harm.
- Valley Forge Christian College v. Americans United for Separation of Church and State, Inc., 454 U.S. 464 (1982), and Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737 (1984): applied; a claimed right to lawful government conduct alone does not satisfy Article III.
- Warth v. Seldin, 422 U.S. 490 (1975), and Sierra Club v. Morton, 405 U.S. 727 (1972): preserved for Congress's power to recognize injuries and broaden protected interests while retaining the plaintiff's own concrete injury.
- Trafficante v. Metropolitan Life Insurance Co., 409 U.S. 205 (1972), and Hardin v. Kentucky Utilities Co., 390 U.S. 1 (1968): distinguished as statutory recognition of concrete personal interests, not authorization of a wholly abstract grievance.

### Standing and representation injury

#### United States Department of Commerce v. Montana, 503 U.S. 442 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-860.
**Decided:** March 31, 1992.

For this case’s other questions, see *United States Department of Commerce v. Montana*, March 31, 1992, No. 91-860: whether equal proportions satisfies the presented interstate population-equality challenge (Election Law — Interstate congressional apportionment); whether congress’s equal-proportions apportionment choice is judicially reviewable (Federal Courts — Political questions and constitutional review); whether count i reversal disposes of the distinct automatic-apportionment claim in count ii (Federal Courts — Scope of appellate disposition).

##### Whether Count I presents a continuing representation injury redressable by review of the allocation formula

**Holding:** On this record, Count I's challenge to the continuing legal allocation of Representatives presents a concrete voting-representation injury traceable to the statutory formula, with a substantial likelihood of redress through adjudication of that formula's validity. Completion of the officials' initial statutory steps does not establish that the allocation has ceased to affect the approaching election or that declaratory relief cannot redress the asserted injury.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Opinion of the Court, Part I, joined by the other eight Justices. All nine participating Justices adopt this proposition at its stated scope; it controls by an express majority, with no judgment-only or narrower-ground vote.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Standing is affirmative and record-specific; it assumes neither a new enactment nor a command to the President, and guarantees no second Montana seat. Count II’s distinct legislative injury is not adjudicated.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse Count I’s judgment, remove its declaration of invalidity and dissolve its permanent injunction, and direct judgment for the Government on Count I only. Remand Count II without deciding it. The allocation remains operative subject to further lawful order; no seat transfer, new enactment or presidential command is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Wesberry v. Sanders, 376 U.S. 1 (1964) — applies its recognition of concrete congressional voting injuries while preserving the distinction between reviewability and appropriate relief; no general rule governing presidential defendants is adopted.

#### Franklin v. Massachusetts, 505 U.S. 788 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1502.
**Decided:** June 26, 1992.

For the constitutional counting question, see Franklin v. Massachusetts, June 26, 1992, No. 91-1502, under Election Law — Interstate congressional apportionment; for finality, statutory authority and administrative review, see Administrative Law — Census determinations and judicial review.

##### Standing for the continuing apportionment injury

**Holding:** A State and its voters have standing to contest inclusion of an identified population in the operative apportionment when that inclusion costs the State a seat and authoritative relief concerning the responsible census determination would likely correct the allocation through the prescribed statutory sequence. Likely redress here does not require an injunction against the President or a general assumption that persons outside the litigation follow judicial advice.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone's opinion of the Court, Parts I–III, joined by the other eight Justices; all nine adopt the inclusion-injury standing holding.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This is the proved injury from inclusion of the overseas population. The Court does not find that a particular alternative residence dataset would restore a seat, establish a general premise that nonparties follow judicial advice, or decide coercive relief against the President.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The District Court's judgment is reversed and its corrective relief dissolved. No replacement allocation, further factual proceedings or injunction against the President is ordered.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States Department of Commerce v. Montana, March 31, 1992, this Court: applied for continuing representational injury and likely declaratory redress through the allocation process; the express unanimous holding remains controlling and its reservation of coercive presidential relief remains intact.
- Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife, June 12, 1992, this Court: applied for injury, causation, likely redress and evidentiary demands; the noncontrolling application concerning absent funding agencies is not promoted into law. Its rule for procedure connected to concrete injury does not require certainty that a new method changes the result.

#### [Johnson v. Miller (Miller v. Johnson / Abrams v. Johnson / United States v. Johnson)](https://github.com/Alex-Oss222/scotus-career/blob/main/terms/OT1994/records/Miller_and_consolidated_merits_1995-06-29.md)

**Docket or dockets:** Nos. 94-631; 94-797; 94-929.
**Decided:** June 29, 1995.

**Companion or consolidated matters:** The appellate alignment is Johnson v. Miller; the three docket aliases identify one constitutional challenge and one reviewed judgment for Georgia.

For the other questions in Johnson v. Miller (Miller v. Johnson / Abrams v. Johnson / United States v. Johnson), decided June 29, 1995, Nos. 94-631; 94-797; 94-929. see Federal Courts — Direct review of three-judge merits judgments (The merits denial of an injunction is directly reviewable); Election Law — Territorial racial design and constitutional deprivation (Racial predominance alone does not establish the asserted constitutional violation; The asserted sorting, status and representation inferences do not establish a distinct deprivation here; Absence of federal compulsion does not itself prohibit voluntary inclusion; District shape is evidence, not a threshold requirement; The supported predominance finding is accepted without dictating its legal consequence).

##### Personal exposure and standing are distinct from success on the merits

**Holding:** Voters personally subjected to the challenged electoral assignment may litigate their asserted unequal treatment when their own exposure, causation and redressability are established, even though their constitutional theory ultimately fails. Residence alone is not automatic standing; the four established Eleventh District plaintiffs have the required personal relationship to the challenged action and relief here.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone, Part I-A, joined by Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: nine votes.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The four established Eleventh District voters seek the same unitary relief; DeLoach's standing need not be decided. Personal exposure establishes a stake, not success on the asserted racial-design theory.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Warth v. Seldin: applies the distinction between personal stake and ultimate substantive entitlement.
- Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife: preserves stage-appropriate factual support for injury, causation and redressability.
- Northeastern Florida Chapter, Associated General Contractors v. Jacksonville and Adarand Constructors v. Peña: apply the separation of personal equality exposure from ultimate success; neither decides the districting merits.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the three-judge district court's judgment for Georgia and denial of injunctive relief in Nos. 94-631, 94-797 and 94-929, without remand. No injunction or redrawing order is unwound and no new injury inquiry, map order, election cancellation or retained supervision is imposed. The judgment does not certify the entire plan or adjudicate separate claims against federal officials.

### Standing to assert third-party rights

#### Nordlinger v. Hahn, 505 U.S. 1 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-1912.
**Decided:** June 18, 1992.

For the acquisition-value and reassessment-exception equal-protection holdings in *Nordlinger v. Hahn*, June 18, 1992, No. 90-1912, see Property and Economic Rights — Property-tax classifications.

##### Whether Nordlinger may obtain heightened review by asserting absent interstate travelers' rights

**Holding:** A taxpayer who alleges no interference with her own interstate movement may not obtain heightened review by asserting absent travelers’ rights when she shows neither a qualifying relationship nor an obstacle to their own litigation. Nordlinger therefore cannot use those unasserted injuries to challenge this scheme; the merits of a proper traveler’s claim remain open.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Part II-A, joined by White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas, controls with seven of nine Justices. Stone-Zsela states the same refusal separately; Stevens does not reach the issue.

**Material application:** Nordlinger already lived in Los Angeles before purchasing her home, alleges no personal impediment to interstate movement and identifies neither a qualifying relationship nor a reason an affected traveler could not sue. Her own financial injury permits her own equal-protection challenge, not assertion of every theory available to another owner.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A properly situated traveler's claim remains open; neither Zobel nor Memorial Hospital is adjudicated on behalf of absent claimants.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The absent-traveler claims are not entertained. Her own equal-protection challenge receives ordinary review.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Allen v. Wright, 468 U.S. 737 — applied to preserve the ordinary limit on asserting others’ rights.
- Warth v. Seldin, 422 U.S. 490 — applied to distinguish personal injury from authority to assert absent persons’ rights.
- Caplin & Drysdale, Chartered v. United States, 491 U.S. 617 — applied; no qualifying relationship or obstacle is established.
- Zobel v. Williams, 457 U.S. 55 — its travel-related protection is not adjudicated on this absent-party claim.
- Memorial Hospital v. Maricopa County, 415 U.S. 250 — its travel-related protection is not adjudicated on this absent-party claim.

#### Georgia v. McCollum, 505 U.S. 42 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-372.
**Decided:** June 18, 1992.

For the state-attribution and Batson-procedure holdings in *Georgia v. McCollum*, June 18, 1992, No. 91-372, see Criminal Procedure — Racial peremptory challenges.

##### Whether the State may assert prospective jurors' equal-protection rights against purposeful racial defense strikes

**Holding:** The State may object to purposeful racial defense strikes in its own criminal trial because discriminatory selection injures the integrity of its tribunal, the State has a direct relationship with summoned jurors, and excluded jurors face substantial barriers to individual litigation. Those circumstances satisfy the ordinary requirements for third-party assertion of their equal-protection rights.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, joined by White, Stevens, Kennedy and Souter; five Justices.

**Material application:** Racial selection injures the integrity of the State's tribunal; the State directly relates to citizens it summons to serve, and excluded jurors face substantial practical obstacles to separate redress.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The holding authorizes supported objections in this criminal jury selection and creates no general state standing to litigate every citizen's constitutional rights. It neither presumes discrimination nor finds a discriminatory strike.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Georgia pretrial judgment is reversed and remanded for jury selection under the constitutional prohibition and ordinary Batson burdens; no conviction or particular juror's seating is adjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Powers v. Ohio, 499 U.S. 400 — applied to the State’s concrete trial-integrity injury, relationship and enforcement obstacle.
- Edmonson v. Leesville Concrete Co., 500 U.S. 614 — applied to the juror relationship and barriers to separate redress.

### State immunity and federal causes of action

#### Hilton v. South Carolina Public Railways Commission, 502 U.S. 197 (1991)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-848.
**Decided:** December 16, 1991.

##### Whether FELA liability of a state-owned interstate railroad remains enforceable in state court

**Holding:** The Federal Employers' Liability Act's established inclusion of state-owned interstate railroads remains enforceable in a competent state court. This rule does not revive constructive waiver of Eleventh Amendment immunity in federal court or establish a general rule for every Article I damages action against a State.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stone, White, Stevens, and Souter; these five Justices supply a majority of the eight participants. Blackmun concurs in the judgment on a different ground and does not supply the controlling rationale.

**Material application:** The retained statutory construction governs the specialized railroad relationship. Congress retained the relevant statutory language, and South Carolina excluded FELA-covered employees from workers' compensation against that established background.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court does not reopen Hilton's dismissed federal suit, decide negligence or damages, or hold that entering commerce generally waives immunity.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The state-court judgment is reversed and the case remanded so the FELA claim may be adjudicated.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Parden's FELA coverage holding is reaffirmed as independent of its constructive federal-forum waiver rationale, which remains displaced by Welch. Welch expressly reserved the statutory coverage question. Will, construing a different statutory term, is distinguished. Howlett is applied: a competent state court may not use state immunity to defeat liability under valid federal law.

### Statutory attorney's fees

#### City of Burlington v. Dague, 505 U.S. 557 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-810.
**Decided:** June 24, 1992.

##### Whether contingency risk permits enhancement under the two environmental fee statutes

**Holding:** A reasonable attorney's fee under 42 U.S.C. §6972(e) and 33 U.S.C. §1365(d) may not be enhanced above a properly determined lodestar to compensate for the risk of nonpayment when compensation depends on success.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia's opinion of the Court, joined by White, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas; five Justices adopt the categorical rule. The dissenters' market-based alternatives supply no controlling exception.

**Current force:** Remains controlling as stated under the two reviewed fee provisions; it is not a prohibition of every possible fee adjustment.

**Material application:** Reasonable hours and rates produced a $198,027.50 lodestar. The $49,506.87 contingency addition, reported as 25%, cannot stand even though the district court found substantial difficulty obtaining counsel without possible enhancement.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court uses reasonable-fee principles shared with similarly worded prevailing-party statutes but adjudicates this award under the two identified provisions. Differently worded compensation schemes, delay adjustments, unreasonable underlying rates and common-fund fees are not decided. Fee eligibility, hours and rates, expenses, state-law damages and the environmental injunction are outside the grant.
